Edited: For formatting and from feedback
So if I am getting this right?

 +-------------------------------+--------------+--------+--------+----------+-------+
 | Lab | XL | L | M+ | M | S/XS |
 +-------------------------------+--------------+--------+--------+----------+-------+
 | Google (Gemini 4 Family #1) | | Argon | | | |
 | Google (Gemini 4 Family #2) | | Carbon | Neon | Hydrogen | |
 | Anthropic (Claude 5.2 Family) | Fable/Mythos | | | | |
 | Anthropic (Claude 5.5 Family) | | Opus | | Sonnet | Haiku |
 | OpenAI (GPT 6.2 Family) | Astra | Sol | | | |
 | OpenAI (GPT 6.1 Family) | | | | Terra | |
 | OpenAI (GPT 6 Family) | | | | | Luna |
 +-------------------------------+--------------+--------+--------+----------+-------+

Notes:

 * Gemini 4 Family
 - Argon -- ++Train --> Carbon
 - Carbon -- Distill --> Neon, Hydrogen
 * Gemini 3 Family
 - 3 Pro -- ++Train --> 3.1 Pro
 - 3 Pro -- Distill --> 3 Flash
 - 3 Flash -- ++Train --> 3.5 Flash ... -- ++Train --> ... 3.8 Flash
 * "S/XS" classification is due to weirdly low pricing for models by Ant/OAI and not by raw perf

locknitpicker [user?id=locknitpicker] 7 hours ago [item?id=50556636] | root [#50556084] | parent [#50556328] | next [#50556558] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

> I'm not sure it even makes sense to align models on price given that Neon appears to be considerably better than Sonnet/Terra.
Models are nowadays compared in terms of their position with regards to a Pareto front assessing traits such as capabilities and cost because there are clear tradeoffs between cost and capabilities.
I'm sure we are going to see speed show up in the analysis in the near future.

Well... This industry moves fast. Carbon had a higher AA score than Astra and Fable when it was announced, though the other labs have updated their models since. From looking at the model selection in Gemini Web and Antigravity:
Flash -> Hydrogen
(medium level) -> Argon -> Neon
Pro -> Carbon
Because Argon and Carbon existed for a while at the same time but treated differently. Pro users get Argon but only Ultra users get Carbon.

impulser_ [user?id=impulser_] 15 hours ago [item?id=50552711] | prev [#50556084] | next [#50552868] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Is there any theory on what is Anthropic's secret sauce? I have been using the latest Gemini models and they just don't seem as good as Opus 5.5 despite the benchmarks. Yeah it comparable on some tasks maybe slightly better but as an overall general intelligent model nothing compared to Opus 5.5 and it hard for me to understand why. Opus feels like human level intelligent like I'm talking to a coworker basically while GPT and Gemini feel like I'm talking to an AI and have to change how I talk. It hard to explain and maybe I'm the only one that feels this.
Is it because Anthropic put a ton more effort into the character of the model that it basically roleplays as a human and that why it tends to think better when giving tasks that it might not be trained heavily on.
I have used Carbon more than most model to try and get the same results but it just feels like i'm talking to a smart AI. Which is fine if you are asking questions, researching an idea, but if you actually want to work on something, build something, it just falls apart. Like Opus can run for days on a task and it feels like it understand what it was doing 2 days ago even when it might not be in it context while other models feel like they breakdown and get lost or falter as the task grows.

 [vote?id=50557352&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50552424#50557352]
relistan [user?id=relistan] 5 hours ago [item?id=50557352] | root [#50552711] | parent [#50553549] | prev [#50553707] | next [#50554231] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

That would be expensive, but there’s a simpler way. First though: I don’t use Argon for that. The cheaper Gemini models are quite good writers, too.
Second: you don’t need to waste a lot of tokens having the other model re-read commits and code. My AGENTS.md/CLAUDE.md specifies to write PR summaries and titles and then hand them off to Gemini to clean them up. Very occasionally this requires two trips if Gemini changes something the other model disagrees with, but usually results in very clear and readable PRs, often better than I would have written if I spent 15 minutes on it. And it uses very few tokens.

 [vote?id=50557306&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50552424#50557306]
nbardy [user?id=nbardy] 5 hours ago [item?id=50557306] | parent [#50552711] | prev [#50552821] | next [#50553034] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I think a lot of it boils down to "constitutional AI".
They did a paper on this a long time ago[1], I'm sure they have altered their techniques some, but I this was the earlier signal that they really treated the AI as a separate entity and trained it as such. And focus not just on it's capability, but it's *behavior*, when to push back on the user, etc... Imo. it's more about the philosophy of the constitution in this paper than the specific RLAIHF, I'm sure this idea of "when to follow instructions and when to trust yourself is in their system prompts for all their RL environments, and even their rewards.
This idea of an objective function vs what the model should do during human interaction is a huge question and a very subtle one to get right, but it's why they were winning in real world application even before they were winning in raw benchmarks.
Especially in the era of RL environments dominating more and more compute it's vey easy to overfit a model to be very good at accomplishing a narrow task exactly as specified. Much more complicated to have it respond appropriately on turn 5 of a nuanced conversation with a user about their private codebase.
[1] https://arxiv.org/abs/2212.08073 [https://arxiv.org/abs/2212.08073]

reply [reply?id=50557306&goto=item%3Fid%3D50552424%2350557306]

 [vote?id=50553034&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50552424#50553034]
foohis [user?id=foohis] 15 hours ago [item?id=50553034] | parent [#50552711] | prev [#50557306] | next [#50557493] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Anthropic spent a lot more time editing their pre-training data with higher-quality material. For example, they used actual books for pre-training, because books are generally run through human editors for higher quality. (this is from the copyright lawsuits)
Other companies would use completely raw internet data for pre-training, which is basically trash. Anthropic's secret sauce is that they filtered and edited down their data for only the highest-quality material. You really don't need quadrillions of tokens to pre-train, as that's just random noise. A smaller-but-higher-quality data set is better in every way.
Meanwhile its obvious that companies like Google would use trash data like email and chat logs or Internet forums for pre-training. Just complete trash data.
This is a classic case of "less is more" and "garbage-in-equals-garbage-out"

reply [reply?id=50553034&goto=item%3Fid%3D50552424%2350553034]

Anthropic doesn't want people to use their models for the chat/persona style use cases OpenAI has spent more time and effort optimizing for.
If you look back at GPT2 and the early days of AI safety/risk this was actually something the kinds of AI researchers that joined Anthropic didn't want - RL on coding->using that accelerate development and to bootstrap capabilities across domains was always the plan, not making it easier for people to spam/fake homework or trap people in problematic human-AI attention/relationship tarpits.
I personally find that Opus 5.5 and Fable 5+ are generally fine to chat with but just very wordy and culturally "LessWrong/essay-guy". Which, if a model is going to have a personality, is close to the one that I want (though it does piss me off when it gets preachy about standards Anthropic clearly doesn't hold themselves against)
I also want to immediately recognize AI generated content on the web or from another person, so I'm very glad it has a distinct voice

 [vote?id=50556781&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50552424#50556781]
foohis [user?id=foohis] 6 hours ago [item?id=50556781] | root [#50552711] | parent [#50556116] | next [#50554760] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Not opinion at all. It's directly from their court case.
"Over time, Anthropic came to value most highly for its data mixes books like the ones Authors had written, and it valued them because of the creative expressions they contained. Claude’s customers wanted Claude to write as accurately and as compellingly as Authors. So, it was best to train the LLMs underlying Claude on works just like the ones Authors had written, with well-curated facts, well-organized analyses, and captivating fictional narratives — above all with “good writing” of the kind “an editor would approve of” (Opp. Exh. 3 at -03433). Anthropic could have trained its LLMs without using such books or any books at all. That would have required spending more on, say, staff writers to create competing exemplars of good writing, engineers to revise bad exemplars into better ones, energy bills to power more rounds of training and fine-tuning, and so on. Having canonical texts to draw upon helped"
From: https://copyrightalliance.org/wp-content/uploads/2025/06/Bar... [https://copyrightalliance.org/wp-content/uploads/2025/06/Bartz-v.-Anthropic-Order.pdf]

 [vote?id=50553592&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50552424#50553592]
Topfi [user?id=Topfi] 14 hours ago [item?id=50553592] | root [#50552711] | parent [#50553034] | prev [#50553822] | next [#50557493] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Purely outside looking in, but yes, I'd argue you are right and higher-quality is a major part of it, both for pre- and post-training. There appears to be a far greater willingness to adopt specifically tailored training sets from third-parties, consistently update and build on them.
From simple frontend work to strategically adding virtual rulers when working with LIDAR scans to extract more accurate data, there are very clear points when Anthropic introduces a new external data set into their mix and while I have seen the same from competitors such as Google, there simply appears to be a greater tendency to stick with these once they have them.
As an example, at release it was quite apparent that Gemini 3 Pro, 3 Flash and Opus 4.5 had a similar, possible even the same frontend-focused training base bought in from a third party. But while Anthropic has had both smaller and larger improvements across each subsequent Opus release, I have not noticed such a consistent improvement with Gemini models over the last year. Maybe Anthropic simply buys updates more often, maybe they create more artificial PRs on top, who knows.
Same with the LIDAR scans, there was a point a few months ago when models started to exhibit a very systematic way of handling visual references in some few leather-crafting tasks, enabling whole new ways of working more efficiently combining existing measurements with images. Step-by-step pixel measurements, compensation for angles and poor captures, etc. Have since seen this in other models, again very much feels like a bought in data set that they are pushing as far as it goes.
Then there is 3D capability with models like Muse Wonder Full. I have not used it myself, if it ain't on API, it doesn't exist, but have seen PRs and do find the model output very parsable, despite it being terrible at any modelling task because these capabilities do not emerge from just training on vast amounts of data, they require high-quality, well tailored, step-by-step reference data of the entire process.
Harness wise, while pure output performance is very consistent across harnesses in my evals (I nowadays use both OpenCode via API and the labs own harness three times each to normalise), Anthropic also appears to be far more willing to use the time between releases to prepare features for the next. Stuff like workflows, proper subagent implementation, advisor, etc. often have found there way into Claude Code either in-between models or were first seen with a model that couldn't fully leverage these. Also feel that explained the good overall launch experience Fable 5 delivered, they simply used the time between announcing Glasswing and launching full access to get the UX/DX polished.
Meanwhile, I am still testing GPT-6.2 Astra and Sol so will refrain from making any more judgement on either till I have all data, but apparently there has been an extensive last minute rewriting of the system prompt to improve performance and "behaviour". Given the gap between GPT-6 Astra and yesterday, along with statements by OpenAI staff alluding that they had this model ready for a while, such things should happen in a far more structured, systematic, well thought out manner, not last second after some negative outside feedback. Heck, from what I know Anthropic, unlike any other lab, does zero early access outside the limited hardening for very exclusive partners like Glasswing. Certainly no early access for regular users, reviewers or the like from what I've heard, so Anthropic does all that prep-work for model releases with less feedback than other labs.

reply [reply?id=50553592&goto=item%3Fid%3D50552424%2350553592]

My theory is firstly its focus - they got to skip to GPT 4 (ie clean room implementation by the same people), they never cared about consumer or video gen or image gen.
Secondly, they somehow made all this work on public clouds with really crappy (compared to baremetal nvidia) networking. I think thats probably a lot of post training (which doesn't need such a large/tightly connected cluster), and some architecture that is less reliant on a tightly connected cluster for pretraining. This would also explain why they have been able to use Trainium, and somehow beat Google while using TPU's.

vintermann [user?id=vintermann] 4 hours ago [item?id=50557587] | parent [#50552711] | prev [#50552927] | next [#50555216] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

There are a lot of real world specialized tasks you can't get better at by logical reasoning or role playing as a human. How you conjugate in some small southeast Asian language. What handwriting looked like in 18th century Norway. These are the tasks which first degrade when you shrink a model. Google's models, in my experience, do well on them, other models not so much.
It makes sense too, because of course Google should have a ton of exotic training data. It's probably their comparative advantage, over getting super-clever with reinforcement learning.

OpenAI has plenty of feedback from Codex. Maybe not quite on the same volume as Claude Codex, but still massive. So I'm not sure that's the whole story.
I doubt there's a single reason for why Anthropic are ahead right now. Building a frontier model requires executing well in a wide variety of things. It's probably a result of being slightly ahead in various aspects of of data collection, model architecture, pre and post training, harness development, etc.
It's a shame that so much of what's happening is being closed doors. Maybe one day, decades from now, somebody (or some AI) will write a definitive history of this period, explaining what was going on at the various labs, how their research compared, and what the major players really thought.

jorl17 [user?id=jorl17] 15 hours ago [item?id=50552816] | parent [#50552711] | prev [#50557275] | next [#50556607] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Here's my average experience with Carbon.
(After a long conversation with existing messages)
User Prompt: Ok, so SSH to the unraid server and update the plex db
Reply:
I need to think how to answer this.
The user is asking me to SSH into the unraid server. This seems to imply there is a machine to which we can SSH to. I should look for places where hosts may exist.
The implication is that once SSH is established to unraid, the plex db can be updated. It seems plex is a service that the machine has in which we can run updates.
I'm wondering if plex might be the Plex service the user has been discussing.
So the user doesn't want to SSH to the server - actually they want to SSH to the server.
... Formulating a plan ...
[some messages later]
Aha! THIS IS THE SMOKING GUN! `~/.ssh/config` HAS THE REQUESTED HOST!!! IT'S TIME TO CONNECT!!
[some messages later]
Ok, time to act. I'll ssh to the server and follow coherent rules.
----
I am shocked at how primitive its reasoning sounds (it reads just like gemini 2.5-pro's old reasoning). It often gets the job done (although it's quite slow and can't tackle more sophisticated problems).
Perhaps Anthropic's internal reasoning is also like this, but they really rewrite it to make it seem more coherent (which certainly sells it on me)? I almost want to click stop just by reading this nonsense.

orbital-decay [user?id=orbital-decay] 14 hours ago [item?id=50553657] | root [#50552711] | parent [#50553474] | next [#50553573] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Neither has any relation to the actual reasoning trace. The summary is actively misleading and you'd be surprised how different it is from the raw CoT of the model. Just ignore it.
If you want to see example traces without jailbreaking claude or gpt, see open models that display the CoT without trying to hide it. It's pretty useful sometimes, with usual caveats (it deceptively looks like English but is heavily reward hacked, and it's generally a weak proxy for model's reasoning process).

Off-topic, but this kind of conversation tells me just how little the average HNer still uses LLMs outside of specific coding tasks, or at least pays attention to them. Which in turn explains so many comments. Probably a good thing though.
You're of course completely right. Interestingly the "summaries" have only become more diverged over time. I now believe the big 3 are barely summarizing at all any more. I deem it likely that the small models don't get the CoT at all, they just completely make up something "plausible" solely based on the user message with a little bit of previous context. Much of the time it will be the complete opposite of something I asked for, or barely related.
Anthropic has changed this up multiple times over the last 12m, I'm talking about the iteration that was around the longest this year. I think they changed it again within the last feww weeks and potentially started actually summarizing again.
Your suggestion is the right one. Everyone who uses LLMs for their job should spend some time with open models like Deepseek and see what the thinking actually looks like.

marmarama [user?id=marmarama] 4 hours ago [item?id=50557559] | root [#50552711] | parent [#50554095] | next [#50556578] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

> Everyone who uses LLMs for their job should spend some time with open models like Deepseek and see what the thinking actually looks like.
I'm not sure DeepSeek 4.x is a good example; its reasoning traces are extremely verbose and detailed. Which is probably one of the reasons it performs so well, and appears to be a deliberate architectural decision to improve skill at the cost of token usage and time-to-answer.
A better comparison might be Gemma or Qwen. Running these locally and with no summarisation, the reasoning traces are not all that different to the big proprietary models in terms of verbosity and content, even if they're not as smart.

declanjackson [user?id=declanjackson] 15 hours ago [item?id=50552868] | prev [#50552711] | next [#50558074] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Something that is interesting is how it's positioned in the overall fleet of Gemini models. Although overall more Intelligent than Carbon, Carbon is still positioned as the 'long horizon' model. This is clear on AA Index, where we see higher overall scores for Neon but lower scores in evals like CritPt which test research-scale reasoning capabilities.
With the frontier getting more jagged, we might see more of this 'less intelligent overall but useful for the frontier tasks'-type models, meaning picking a model will only get more difficult from here.
(FD: I work at AA)

 [vote?id=50553443&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50552424#50553443]
seizethecheese [user?id=seizethecheese] 14 hours ago [item?id=50553443] | parent [#50552868] | prev [#50553291] | next [#50552897] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Okay, this "better at long horizon tasks" thing might explain why Carbon scores worse on most benchmarks yet is the larger model.
I looked into this using data I have from a battle system built on top of OpenCode (so we're trying to capture real world use). It looks like Carbon actually does a lot better when it uses significantly more output than opponents.
Win rate when output is less than 2.5x opponents: 53% (41/78)
Win rate when output is more than 2.5x opponents: 73% (41/56)
The p-val on this is 0.02. Data from https://agentduel.com/leaderboard [https://agentduel.com/leaderboard].
There doesn't seem to be any relationship between actual size of task though. Win rate appears random based on total output of Carbon and also opponents. (Though our data is still small, so it might exist.)
Looks basically like Carbon was trained to think a lot longer, not necessarily that it's better on large tasks.

reply [reply?id=50553443&goto=item%3Fid%3D50552424%2350553443]

This comment always comes up but isn’t this literally irreducibly complex in the sense of Kolmogorov complexity?
I’m out of my depth here but it does seem prompts have a “smell” to their complexity that means we can use a classifier to mate them to a model with better success than random noise, but still don’t essentially all of the savings associated with systems like this just come because there is some “complexity arbitrage” where there is enough of a token cost differential between models that you always save money by trying the cheap model first and then trying a hard model if it turns out to be hard?

 [vote?id=50556703&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50552424#50556703]
Gareth321 [user?id=Gareth321] 7 hours ago [item?id=50556703] | root [#50552868] | parent [#50553365] | next [#50553020] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I’m also not an expert but that sounds a lot like the toilet seat problem. Mathematically it’s preferable to just always leave the seat as you used it, instead of using effort to return it to some desired state. This minimises effort in aggregate for all parties. I think it’s possible that routing every request through a simpler model first ends up more expensive in the long run, depending on the cost of that simple model. And if it’s too simple, the routing inefficiencies and mistakes will produce worse results.
I think one of the more difficult problems with this is that the complexity is often unclear at first. It’s not until some thinking and analysis that the complexity emerges. It’s just not possible for a simple model to deduce this, and we don’t want a dumb model investigating a complex issue.
I think in the medium term we end up with large models which are efficient with simple queries. Meaning very few tokens used to answer simple queries, keeping costs low. So one just uses this large model for everything.
Longer term I think LLMs move closer to the structure of the brain, whereby different regions/models have specialised functionality, and they communicate and coordinate depending on the task. For example, telemetry doesn’t need to pass through the prefrontal cortex. It just goes straight to the telemetry model which is super simple and cheap.

refulgentis [user?id=refulgentis] 15 hours ago [item?id=50553115] | root [#50552868] | parent [#50552969] | prev [#50553470] | next [#50555844] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

There's stuff that does this and I've put a substantial amount of time last couple months looking into it, unfortunately you always, always, end up at chicken-and-egg problems.
It's really frustrating to describe. Easiest is you can't score on just the prompt, you need the full context. Ex. "Solve Fermat's last theorem" initial prompt then "continue" followup prompt. Which gets you back in the tarpit of "if I have to pay a model to do an inference run on context, then, the context is cached and I should continue using that model."
The vision of another model thats RL maxx'd on knowing how to delegate has a few problems. One, we can observe externally, it died/didn't work even at OpenAI's scale and willingness to make it work, i.e. the...GPT-5.0? IIRC...router that silently went away. The other, if such a model was built, it'd also be good at regulating it's own thinking enough that it'd naturally use less tokens.
In practice, the frontier hackers have settled on a decision model (i.e. Jev) x calibrating effort level or choosing from a set of models x reasoning effort tuples they specify rules for choosing in their Jev prompt. My guess is this stays stable and the only real choice.

Carbon has a huge problem that it's just way too slow, and outputs way too much per task (on real world tasks, it's the worst at this https://micjm.com/blog/carbon-is-that-slow/ [https://micjm.com/blog/carbon-is-that-slow/]).

AA index doesn't tell you much. Some small models with low AA index are still incredibly useful because you know they can stay coherent and behave well over long tasks, and use your harness effectively despite the lower supposed "intelligence".
Some giant models overengineer things and add redundant checks everywhere instead of simplifying overall design. In humans, that'd be a sign of low intelligence.

Google has fully moved to rolling releases paradigm as their internal RSI starts to work, we'll see more rapid releases that improves on all fronts(size and capacity) and it starts to confuse the model size paradigm.
You can often have a new set of RL envs or RL algos that train a smaller model past the capacity of the bigger model, especially with distillation involved and the tradeoffs of RL rollouts vs pretrain.

dannyw [user?id=dannyw] 15 hours ago [item?id=50552756] | root [#50552524] | parent [#50552651] | next [#50552847] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

A Microsoft Azure page briefly claimed Sol runs with less looped transformer passes; and so you could speculate that Sol and Astra might share weights; but have different “loop” factors.
That would make a lot of sense from an inference optimisation perspective. The same prefill and decode machines can hold one set of weights, and serve both Astra and Sol requests.
At (multi-)terabyte-scale model weights, and when you have to offer not just the latest like Fable 5.2 but still serve 5.1, 5.0, etc; heck I think Opus 4.6 is still around; you really care about overall compute utilisation.
Idle GPUs are wasted GPUs; and even over the fastest interlinks you’re not hotswapping multi terabytes of weights.
With MoEs, there’s probably stuff you can play around with varying the number of active experts; or other clever optimisations.
Maybe Sonnet 5.5 could be just Opus 5.5 with a different router and less active params? Who knows; I’m just theorycrafting now :)

 [vote?id=50552937&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50552424#50552937]
onlyrealcuzzo [user?id=onlyrealcuzzo] 15 hours ago [item?id=50552937] | root [#50552524] | parent [#50552756] | next [#50552792] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

> A Microsoft Azure page briefly claimed Sol runs with less looped transformer passes;
This doesn't really make sense given how the models released.
OpenAI released Astra long before latent-space looping was a thing at the frontier space.
Then they released Sol 6, which was fast, clearly not looped. Then they release Sol 6.1 which was nearly as good as Astra, but way cheaper, and WAY slower -> this would follow it just being a looped version of Sol 6.
It doesn't seem likely that Astra 6.2 is just a looped Astra 6... It's too fast, and they'd go bankrupt if they're charging the same price... It may be a further looped version of Sol than Sol 6.2 - which would explain why it's not as much better as we would've hoped.
It may also explain why their release is not going as well as they hoped. It's hard to predict how your first looped models are going to fare.
If you look at how long it took Google to recover from their failed 3.5 Pro, it would be surprising if GPT recovered from their rumored failed run on Astra in under a month. That would give them reason to release a looped Sol and call it Astra.

Fable & Opus, Astra & Sol, and Carbon are all thought to be >1T param models - AFAIK, most people think Opus & Sol are ~5T param models and Astra, Fable are 10T+.
I think the rumor-mill consensus is that Google's models are significantly smaller while attempting to swing at the same level - but the ratios AFAIK are thought to be around the same - i.e. Neon is somewhere around half as big as Carbon, big enough that a newer checkpoint can make it temporarily a little bit better.

weitendorf [user?id=weitendorf] 15 hours ago [item?id=50553263] | root [#50552524] | parent [#50552651] | prev [#50552847] | next [#50552900] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

There's only a limited amount of hardware for training and serving models at scale, and which form an upper bound for what model size/inference can support.
A GB300 rack has 72x288GB=20TB VRAM. Do the math and 10T parameter models are just about the max size you can easily train and serve (at 1M context) without doing a lot of custom hardware development or limiting your scale.

Is Carbon 6x the size of Hydrogen and a third the size of Argon? When they release Xeon can we make compounds? Feels like some really cheap Da Vinci Code esque thing if we're supposed to get some sort of meaning out of the names.

aenis [user?id=aenis] 5 hours ago [item?id=50557486] | root [#50552524] | parent [#50556234] | next [#50552848] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Gold plating for tasks where the agent is given the intention, the explicit ask to make the best impression, and ways of self evaluating. Much like openai's agents pre 6.2 sol/astra, it considers the task satisfactorily completed a bit earlier than anthropic models which seem to better guess what the specs left out. It is a useless feature for those who keep their llms at a short leash - my hobby benchmark aims to evaluate performance at under-specified tasks and, well, vibe coding as it was described a year ago.
It's a good model for delegating work to, but not deciding the detailed scope. So, not fable class for me.

Mogzol [user?id=Mogzol] 15 hours ago [item?id=50552724] | parent [#50552524] | prev [#50552581] | next [#50552615] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

As far as I can tell it's arbitrary and Google is just choosing element names they think sound good.
When Argon first came out it seemed like they could be going for noble gasses, like Helium (small), Neon (mid), Argon (big).
But then Carbon came out and it seemed like maybe they were going through the alphabet for model generations. Argon, Boron (internal), Carbon.
And now they've come out with Hydrogen and Neon, and there's no logical way to order them. The actual model sizes are Hydrogen (small), Neon (mid), Carbon (big). This doesn't match sorting by atomic number or alphabetically.

reply [reply?id=50552724&goto=item%3Fid%3D50552424%2350552724]

 [vote?id=50552615&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50552424#50552615]
WarmWash [user?id=WarmWash] 15 hours ago [item?id=50552615] | parent [#50552524] | prev [#50552724] | next [#50552839] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

They are almost certainly being ambiguous so that people rely on the model descriptors to pick which one to use.
I think there are a ton of people who automatically default to the largest model with the highest thinking for everything. Which historically made sense but not nearly as much anymore.
At this point a model like hydrogen can cover just about everything an average person is doing. You don't need Carbon deploying an agent swarm pushing terabytes of parameters through HBM memory to help you plan your birthday party.

They use the very precise "it sounds cool". Neon is 75% the price of Carbon but has 4 extra protons in the nucleus and is also a noble gas. Argon is a heavier noble gas at 16 protons.
element/model - atomic number - cost
H - 1 - $1.50 & 7.50 per mtok
C - 6 - $4 & 20 per mtok
Ne - 10 - $3 & 15 per mtok
Ar - 16 - $2 & 10 per mtok
Claude - length of story but fable messes it up
Haiku - 3 lines
Sonnet - 14 lines
Opus - a novel?
Fable - 30 lines??
Mythos 15700 lines?
Luna - 28 days
Sol - 365 days
Astra - 5-6000 stars

> And hey, if you find yourself lost, the periodic table is probably the most ubiquitous ordered list in the world and serves to clear things up pretty fast.
But the way google ranks the models by capability doesn't follow periodic table order.
Hydrogen - 1
Carbon - 6
Neon - 10
Argon - 18
It could very well be based on their density (g/cm^3 in standard conditions):
Hydrogen - 0.0000899
Neon - 0.000900
Argon - 0.001784
Carbon - 2.267
This would make more sense than assuming it is based on their periodic table order as it more accurately maps to how Google markets their capabilities, but is too esoteric to really be useful for clearing up confusion for normal users, IMO.

 [vote?id=50557488&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50552424#50557488]
bertmuir [user?id=bertmuir] 5 hours ago [item?id=50557488] | root [#50552524] | parent [#50553029] | next [#50552867] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I think it's more vibes-based. The different periodic groups have different meaning.
Neon and Argon are descending down the noble gases, I'd expect their successors are Krypton, Xenon, Radon.
They skipped Helium and went straight to Neon, which makes me think they've reserved Helium as a lightweight element, and are going to follow the lightest elements for the cheapest models. Hydrogen, Helium, Lithium, and so on.
Carbon reads more as a "foundation of life" kind of metaphorical choice, and they have some choices in the periodic groups nearby to keep that sort of metaphor for a while. My guess is they move horizontally - Nitrogen, Oxygen - then on to Silicon when they hit something appropriately transformative with the model.
I'd expect the most experimental or fastest models to eventually release on highly reactive groups - Fluorine, Sodium, etc. - and that they're leaving the metallic elements in the middle for differentiation in industry-specific fine-tuned models.

reply [reply?id=50557488&goto=item%3Fid%3D50552424%2350557488]

wat10000 [user?id=wat10000] 15 hours ago [item?id=50552867] | root [#50552524] | parent [#50552749] | prev [#50553029] | next [#50555122] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

The periodic table isn't really helping. Hydrogen is the lightest, that much makes sense. Beyond that it doesn't seem to work. The elemental ordering is H C Ne Ar. The cost ordering is H Ne Ar C (with Ar and C being the same per token but I gather that C thinks more and thus costs more). The capability ordering seems to be H Ar C Ne. Argon is the highest on the table but is neither the most expensive nor the smartest.

 [vote?id=50552642&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50552424#50552642]
bemtevi [user?id=bemtevi] 15 hours ago [item?id=50552642] | prev [#50552548] | next [#50552877] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

If one day, AI becomes provably good at all programming tasks, at working at mathematics better than all mathematicians, and at solving problems in almost all domains with maximal efficiency, I think it'd be a dystopia.
What I simply don't get is why people want to push the boundaries of efficiency and knowledge. I used to think as a teenager that all knowledge was good but how good is knowledge when we can't acquire wisdom with a comparable speed?
The greatest thing about the early internet and earlier science was that people shared and communicated with each other. Now they just share what they produce wihouth much humanity at all.
It seems to me that the creation of all these models has just become a race where no one really cares about the finish line and no one really understands why we're doing it, except for the short-term trail of dollar bills on the race course.
For me, each new model release is a sad day for humanity.

reply [reply?id=50552642&goto=item%3Fid%3D50552424%2350552642]

 [vote?id=50557546&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50552424#50557546]
bertmuir [user?id=bertmuir] 4 hours ago [item?id=50557546] | parent [#50552642] | next [#50557388] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Because hundreds of millions of people live in abject poverty, and if we divvied our economic production up evenly today there'd only be ~$5,000 to ~$15,000 per capita at a US cost of living i.e. the global economy is far too small for everyone to have good lives.
The growth mindset isn't really for you, if you're already rich, and don't want to be richer. It's coming from the vast majority of the world, which is poor, and the minority that is rich but wants to get richer.

 [vote?id=50554842&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50552424#50554842]
mrandish [user?id=mrandish] 12 hours ago [item?id=50554842] | root [#50553269] | parent [#50554168] | next [#50555095] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

True story about Sony naming: I was at the PC industry trade show where Sony launched the new VAIO computer in a big, beautiful booth at the front of the hall. Being a long-time Sony fan, near the end of the first day after the crowds had mostly cleared, I stopped by the Sony booth on my way out to see the new PC. I saw that the VAIO name also formed the computer's distinctive ultra-modern logo featured in large bold text on each side of the PC's futuristic case in a minimalist block font. But I was puzzled how a company with a history of so many great products with awesome names (WalkMan, DiscMan, PlayStation) had come up with such an awful name.
Since I was curious about the origin of the name, I wandered over to where three Sony employees were chatting passing time until the day ended. I learned all three were mid-level marketing staff based in Sony's New Jersey US HQ. So I asked "How do you pronounce the computer's name?" All three had different takes between various long vowel vs short vowel sounds.
Since they were all facing me, I also asked, "Without turning around, how do you spell the name?" They all paused, then laughed. They did manage to work that out after a few false starts but it wasn't exactly smooth. As we chatted some more I asked who'd come up with such a unique name and learned Sony had hired a "really cool" high-end branding agency who specializes in coming up with great product names. The effort and expense in creating the name and logo had clearly been marketed to employees as "cutting-edge cool." I warmly wished them the best with the new product before wandering out of the hall to dinner.

reply [reply?id=50554842&goto=item%3Fid%3D50552424%2350554842]

None of it true of course. The VAIO name and logo was by Gotō Teiyū who had been working as a designer at Sony for 30 years at that point. Its pronounciation is quite unequivocal in Japanese as well (like bio).

Well, I guess the mid-level marketing people in Sony's New Jersey office got some garbled version of what happened in Japan, because that's what they told me at PC Expo '96 at the Javitz Center.
Regardless, the PC and it's neat industrial design plus Sony's brand and distribution were good enough to overcome a weak name. VAIO was an acronym for "Video Audio Input Output" (which Sony later changed to "Visual Audio Intelligence Organizer", although I'm not sure why they thought that would be better).

There is definitely a system. They are training models of different sizes for different levels of inference efficiency, which maps to token pricing. The ordered set is the size of the weights/ computational complexity during inference.

ijidak [user?id=ijidak] 9 hours ago [item?id=50555903] | root [#50553269] | parent [#50553607] | prev [#50555095] | next [#50555930] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I feel Anthropic and OpenAI are actually doing a great job with their naming.
We intuitively understand that Luna is smaller than Terra, and Terra is smaller than Sol, and Sol is smaller than Astra.
Likewise, we intuitively understand that a haiku is shorter than a sonnet, and a sonnet is shorter than an opus, and an opus is shorter than a fable. Fable is probably the weakest of their names. Maybe they should have gone with Epic.
It's Google that isn't doing it right.
Even those who know physics have zero intuition regarding carbon, argon, and neon.
Hydrogen being the smallest makes sense.
But what property is the point of comparison for carbon, argon, and neon?
Valence shell? Atomic mass? I saw someone throw out density.
It provides no self-evident point of comparison.
Frankly, if they can't even get naming right, they're likely in trouble on the productization side.
It reminds me of Microsoft in the Zune era.
They just don't have what it takes to win on the product side in this battle.

It makes sense if you understand that the size of a model is something important and meaningfully different than just how advanced the model is.
You could argue that's a detail the user shouldn't have to concern themselves with, but I think it's appropriate to expect them to learn that and pick names which put that concept front and center. The various "model router" approaches for consumer tools like ChatGPT have been finicky and inappropriate for professional tools we spend a lot of money on.

They should standardize on naming like Medals for all models.
Medal - Anthropic Claude, OpenAI, Google Gemini , Meta Muse
Bronze - Haiku , Luna , Hydrogen, Ember
Silver - Sonnet , Terra, Argon (Now defunct) , Spark
Gold - Opus , Sol, Neon, Wonder
Platinum - Mythos/Fable, Astra , Carbon, NA
Diamond - ?
One flaw is people using it will always try to stay on gold or above - in their mind, gold might be better so they may spend more when it’s not needed.
Silver is all you need now for most of coding work.
Edit: updated argon and neon categories on table above as Neon replaced Argon

 [vote?id=50554392&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50552424#50554392]
B-Con [user?id=B-Con] 13 hours ago [item?id=50554392] | parent [#50553269] | prev [#50554796] | next [#50554442] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I'm terrible with names, so I'm in the same boat. There's a lot of marketing going on.
But the naming isn't completely random, here's what I use to make the ordered list in my head:
Claude is named after creative works, and uses bigger works for more powerful models. In order of strength:
* Haiku - short poem (we all know this one)
* Sonnet - "medium" length poem (many of us know this one)
* Opus - It's the singular form of "opera", so it's like a stand-alone segment of a larger body of work. (Nobody knew this one, but not hard to remember, and know you know that "opera" is actually plural! The description sounds bigger than a sonnet, but it's not a strict ordering.)
* Fable - a story (this one doesn't seem obviously bigger than an opus, but I guess an opus is one part and a fable is a front-to-end story, it works)
* Mythos - another type of story, which works and it's basically Fable but Anthropic's internal-only version
Gemini, as of version 4, started doing something with atoms:
* Hydrogen - smallest periodic element, the most common gas in outer space
* Neon - a bigger element, less common in outer space
* Argon - yet bigger element, yet less common in outer space (as best I can tell, I am not an astronomer)
* Carbon - this breaks that pattern, its a smaller element than the first three, and not a gas... but critical for life on this planet? And it sounds like a longer version of Argon. So I can remember it, but I'm not sure what the intended pattern was.
The model ordering is not strictly upgrades, but AFAIK they are intended to be steps up in terms of (claimed) complexity and reasoning power. But since it's an evolving field, the specifics of how cost, speed, and accuracy combine can make it hard to know how to order a given model for your use case. (i.e. cheap & fast with a harness that makes more calls, or expensive and slow but better at one-shot).
Deepseek's naming is more organized and literal, but you still have to remember the prefixes for V = version (plain) and R = Reasoning, and that they have different specialized models that aren't intended to be compared to each other.

reply [reply?id=50554392&goto=item%3Fid%3D50552424%2350554392]

 [vote?id=50555256&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50552424#50555256]
adrianmonk [user?id=adrianmonk] 11 hours ago [item?id=50555256] | root [#50553269] | parent [#50554392] | next [#50554595] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I'm not sure if it's intentional, but the Google AI code names are all elements used in producing light.
* Neon and Argon: noble gases commonly used in gas-discharge lamps[1].
* Carbon: used in arc lamps[2] (the first practical form of artificial electric light).
* Hydrogen: the sun (abundant light, available to everyone).
The symbolism of light would make sense for AI. Shedding light on a subject, enlightenment, a light bulb moment, etc.
If this is right, then maybe we'll see krypton, xenon, mercury, sodium, or tungsten next.
---
[1] https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gas-discharge_lamp [https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gas-discharge_lamp]
[2] https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arc_lamp [https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arc_lamp]

reply [reply?id=50555256&goto=item%3Fid%3D50552424%2350555256]

 [vote?id=50554595&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50552424#50554595]
surround [user?id=surround] 12 hours ago [item?id=50554595] | root [#50553269] | parent [#50554392] | prev [#50555256] | next [#50554471] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Basically every lab has a model that doesn't fit their naming scheme 100%.
OpenAI's naming scheme is in Latin:
* Luna - Moon (smallest)
* Terra - Earth (medium)
* Sol - Sun (large)
* Astra - Star (could be larger than the sun I guess)
Meta's Muse:
* Ember - (smallest)
* Spark - (medium)
* Wonder - (large)
A spark is the beginning of an idea whereas wonder is associated deep thought. But an ember and a spark are both small pieces of fire, so the ordering is a little muddy.
Google's naming scheme has the least clear pattern of any lab.

nl [user?id=nl] 11 hours ago [item?id=50555341] | parent [#50553269] | prev [#50554442] | next [#50556175] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I think vendors assume their names schemes are obvious.
Anthropic: "We'll name after the size of literature style! An opus is obviously bigger than a sonnet!"
Much of the world: "What is this word Opus?"
OpenAI: "We'll use the Latin names for astronomical bodies. Everyone knows that terra = earth and luna = the moon and that the earth is bigger than the moon."
Much of the world: "Does terra mean it is terrified? Oh well at least it is better than Gpt 4o vs Gpt o3"
Google: "We'll name it Bard! Oh, that was bad. Lets name it Gemini because that sounds cool. Oh we need different models, ok we'll have Pro for the big expensive on, and Flash for the fast one. But now we want a fast large one and also we have mid sized models. Ok, lets name them after elements, but not ones that anyone knows anything about so now we can confuse them as to if Neon or Carbon or Argon is better. But Gemini Hydrogen is the cheap fast one"
Everyone: "Ok, Gemini Hydrogen is cheap. But this is too confusing, I'll just use Claude".

What do Corolla, Camry, Crown mean?
While cutesy, most current models are quasi-semantic progressions.
Claude (length, or cultural durability): Haiku, Sonnet, Opus, Fable
GPT (altitude): Terra, Luna, Sol, Astra
---
Gemini unfortunately has no semantic key Hydrogen, Carbon, Argon (superseded by Carbon), Neon.

ijidak [user?id=ijidak] 9 hours ago [item?id=50555875] | parent [#50553269] | prev [#50553525] | next [#50553570] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Well Anthropic and OpenAI are actually doing a great job with their naming.
We intuitively understand that Luna is smaller than Terra, and Terra is smaller than Sol, and Sol is smaller than Astra.
Likewise, we intuitively understand that a haiku is shorter than a sonnet, and a sonnet is shorter than an opus, and an opus is shorter than a fable. Fable is probably the weakest of their names. Maybe they should have gone with Epic.
It's Google that isn't doing it right.
Even the educated have no intuition regarding carbon, argon, and neon.
Hydrogen being the smallest makes sense.
But what property is the point of comparison for carbon argon and neon?
Valence shell? Atomic mass?
It provides no self-evident point of comparison. It's absolutely atrocious.

reply [reply?id=50555875&goto=item%3Fid%3D50552424%2350555875]

lucumo [user?id=lucumo] 11 hours ago [item?id=50555115] | root [#50553269] | parent [#50554005] | next [#50554050] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

A fable is just a story with antropomorphized animals and a moral. They can get very short.
Here's a translation of the entire Fox and Grapes fable:
> Driven by hunger, a fox tried to reach some grapes hanging high on the vine but was unable to, although he leaped with all his strength. As he went away, the fox remarked "Oh, you aren't even ripe yet! I don't need any sour grapes." People who speak disparagingly of things that they cannot attain would do well to apply this story to themselves.

reply [reply?id=50555115&goto=item%3Fid%3D50552424%2350555115]

Yes, but such stories with behavioural learnings appear across countries and cultures in various forms, e.g. the animals are swapped for more local ones, the learning applies to a more culturally common behaviour, etc.

Prior knowledge of common poetic forms taught in middle school?
The point is that there is prior knowledge that completely explains the entire system. A 3 line poem < a 14 line poem < a book-sized work < a multi-book epic, boom done, easy. Consistent and friendly using words that everyone should know.
With google we have element 1 < element 18 < element 10 < element 6. There is no consistent schema where their namesakes map to their relative sizes, price, and capability.

It's important to note that DeepMind and the other two labs aren't playing the same game.
The latter need to raise funds and IPO, but the former is an arm of one of the most powerful companies to ever exist with some of the largest data warehouses around and custom hardware. And they have an endless pile of internal code to maintain already with hundreds of millions of end users already bought-in.
The incentives being different leads to different focuses and product lifecycles.

jaykru [user?id=jaykru] 15 hours ago [item?id=50552987] | prev [#50553896] | next [#50553968] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Carbon 4 has been incredible for my use, tuning a local inference engine based on tinygrad running on Tenstorrent hardware. I had a barely functional prototype I built with Claude that petered out at an unusable level of performance. Astra and Sol didn't make much progress when I pointed them at it. Carbon 4 more than 10x'd the prefill and doubled the decode performance on Qwen 3.8 27B without breaking quality or the generality of the stack. Very nice to see competition at the frontier again.

jaykru [user?id=jaykru] 14 hours ago [item?id=50553492] | root [#50552987] | parent [#50553182] | next [#50555940] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I'm running 2x p150a's right now. I have them hooked up to a mini-ITX PC with a single PCIe slot via a quad x1 switch (figuring out power to wire up the other two cards I own). My best numbers so far are around 2500t/s prefill about 52t/s decode with DFlash2. All of this is vibecoded besides the original design sketch which was maybe a 3 line description of what tweaks to make to tinygrad and which parts of the existing TT stack to use (almost nothing).

API prices are arbitrarily set though. Google set their pricing to control the demand rather than because it had to be that high.
I heard that Google share price doesn't really depend on how much their models are used. But rather how do the bench and whether they are theoretically publicly accessible so that their quality can be independantly confirmed. So Google can price their model higher to get most of the benefits without having to actually provide too much inference.
They are probably releasing the models to the public at all only because they got so much backlash after witholding Argon.

mertarican [user?id=mertarican] 14 hours ago [item?id=50553711] | prev [#50552701] | next [#50557132] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Well, I use my product as a benchmark for this new models and I have to say that Argon is still the most efficient model by far for my use case. Carbon, Hydrogen, and now Neon all use 3 to 4 times higher tokens compared to Argon for creating demos. Neon was the first Google model that passed 8192 MAX OUTPUT TOKEN limit, which is disappointing a little bit. But lot's of experience I got from these models, for example bigger models like Argon spend significantly less output token so even though unit cost is higher both total cost and the output time can be lower than the lower models.

 [vote?id=50554910&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50552424#50554910]
Destructotor [user?id=Destructotor] 12 hours ago [item?id=50554910] | parent [#50553600] | next [#50554463] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Looks to me like just another math benchmark similar to FrontierMath.
> We introduce Riemann-Bench, a private benchmark of expert-curated problems designed to evaluate AI systems on research-level mathematics that goes far beyond the olympiad frontier. Problems are authored by Ivy League mathematics professors, graduate students, and PhD-holding IMO medalists, and routinely took their authors weeks to solve independently. Each problem undergoes double-blind verification by two independent domain experts who must solve the problem from scratch, and yields a unique, closed-form solution assessed by programmatic verifiers.
https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.06802 [https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.06802]

minimumvector [user?id=minimumvector] 10 hours ago [item?id=50555375] | prev [#50552665] | next [#50552503] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

On AA's Intelligence Index (https://artificialanalysis.ai/ [https://artificialanalysis.ai/]), the ranking is Fable > Astra > Neon > Opus > Sol.
On Vals's Index (https://www.vals.ai/benchmarks/vals_index [https://www.vals.ai/benchmarks/vals_index]), it's Fable > Neon > Sonnet > Opus > ... > Astra.
On Surge's Tuesday@Work Index (https://surgehq.ai/benchmarks [https://surgehq.ai/benchmarks]), it's Neon > Fable > Astra > Opus > Sol.
Overall, it seems like Gemini is back at the frontier with Anthropic and OpenAI, and well ahead of Meta and Grok. Despite what folks were claiming a couple months ago - when after Jeff Dean and Noam Shazeer left, they were saying Gemini would never be SOTA again.
Once Anthropic and OpenAI are public, I wonder how new releases like this will affect their stock prices?

The names just keep getting more random. And the table at the bottom, I'm pretty sure it's conventional (at least in the English speaking part of the world which I'm not originally from myself) to put your plan/model/price/whatever tiers left to right in increasing rate order. They do right to left here. Are they deliberately trying to confuse people? I get it that we tech workers are generally wary of google, microsoft, etc. naming conventions often (always) being terrible, but the average user out there (which increasingly is everyone with a smartphone or PC) could just skim through the announcement and go away with an easily avoidable misunderstanding.
